Due to the sensitive dependence of solutions on initial conditions (SDIC), also known as the butterfly effect, chaotic systems like the Lorenz 1963 model imply a finite predictability horizon. This means that while accurate predictions are possible over a finite time period, they are not feasible over an infinite time span. Considering the nature of Lorenz's chaotic solutions, the committee led by Charney et al. in 1966[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-136) extrapolated a doubling time of five days from a general circulation model, suggesting a predictability limit of two weeks. This connection between the five-day doubling time and the two-week predictability limit was also recorded in a 1969 report by the Global Atmospheric Research Program (GARP).[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-137) To acknowledge the combined direct and indirect influences from the Mintz and Arakawa model and Lorenz's models, as well as the leadership of Charney et al., Shen et al.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-138) refer to the two-week predictability limit as the "Predictability Limit Hypothesis," drawing an analogy to Moore's Law.

Other areas

# Other areas

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Chaos_theory&action=edit&section=24)]

In chemistry, predicting gas solubility is essential to manufacturing [polymers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymers), but models using [particle swarm optimization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_swarm_optimization) (PSO) tend to converge to the wrong points. An improved version of PSO has been created by introducing chaos, which keeps the simulations from getting stuck.[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-139) In [celestial mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_mechanics), especially when observing asteroids, applying chaos theory leads to better predictions about when these objects will approach Earth and other planets.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-140) Four of the five [moons of Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Pluto) rotate chaotically. In [quantum physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_physics) and [electrical engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_engineering), the study of large arrays of [Josephson junctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephson_junctions) benefitted greatly from chaos theory.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrogatz2003[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_March_2026]]%3Csup_class=%22noprint_Inline-Template_%22_style=%22white-space:nowrap;%22%3E&#91;%3Ci%3E[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|%3Cspan_title=%22This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&#32;(March_2026)%22%3Epage&nbsp;needed%3C/span%3E]]%3C/i%3E&#93;%3C/sup%3E-57) Closer to home, coal mines have always been dangerous places where frequent natural gas leaks cause many deaths. Until recently, there was no reliable way to predict when they would occur. But these gas leaks have chaotic tendencies that, when properly modeled, can be predicted fairly accurately.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-141)

Chaos theory can be applied outside of the natural sciences, but historically nearly all such studies have suffered from lack of reproducibility; poor external validity; and/or inattention to cross-validation, resulting in poor predictive accuracy (if out-of-sample prediction has even been attempted). Glass[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-142) and Mandell and Selz[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-143) have found that no EEG study has as yet indicated the presence of strange attractors or other signs of chaotic behavior.

Redington and Reidbord (1992) attempted to demonstrate that the human heart could display chaotic traits. They monitored the changes in between-heartbeat intervals for a single psychotherapy patient as she moved through periods of varying emotional intensity during a therapy session. Results were admittedly inconclusive. Not only were there ambiguities in the various plots the authors produced to purportedly show evidence of chaotic dynamics (spectral analysis, phase trajectory, and autocorrelation plots), but also when they attempted to compute a Lyapunov exponent as more definitive confirmation of chaotic behavior, the authors found they could not reliably do so.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-144)

In their 1995 paper, Metcalf and Allen[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-145) maintained that they uncovered in animal behavior a pattern of period doubling leading to chaos. The authors examined a well-known response called schedule-induced polydipsia, by which an animal deprived of food for certain lengths of time will drink unusual amounts of water when the food is at last presented. The control parameter (r) operating here was the length of the interval between feedings, once resumed. The authors were careful to test a large number of animals and to include many replications, and they designed their experiment so as to rule out the likelihood that changes in response patterns were caused by different starting places for r.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed)*]

Time series and first delay plots provide the best support for the claims made, showing a fairly clear march from periodicity to irregularity as the feeding times were increased. The various phase trajectory plots and spectral analyses, on the other hand, do not match up well enough with the other graphs or with the overall theory to lead inexorably to a chaotic diagnosis. For example, the phase trajectories do not show a definite progression towards greater and greater complexity (and away from periodicity); the process seems quite muddied. Also, where Metcalf and Allen saw periods of two and six in their spectral plots, there is room for alternative interpretations. All of this ambiguity necessitate some serpentine, post-hoc explanation to show that results fit a chaotic model.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed)*]

By adapting a model of career counseling to include a chaotic interpretation of the relationship between employees and the job market, Amundson and Bright found that better suggestions can be made to people struggling with career decisions.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-146) Modern organizations are increasingly seen as open [complex adaptive systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_adaptive_system) with fundamental natural nonlinear structures, subject to internal and external forces that may contribute chaos. For instance, [team building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_building) and [group development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_development) is increasingly being researched as an inherently unpredictable system, as the uncertainty of different individuals meeting for the first time makes the trajectory of the team unknowable.[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-147)

 [![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/BML_N%3D200_P%3D32.png/250px-BML_N%3D200_P%3D32.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BML_N=200_P=32.png)The [BML traffic model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BML_traffic_model): The red cars and blue cars take turns to move; the red ones only move upwards, and the blue ones move rightwards. Every time, all the cars of the same colour try to move one step if there is no car in front of it. Here, the model has self-organized in a somewhat geometric pattern where there are some traffic jams and some areas where cars can move at top speed.

Traffic forecasting may benefit from applications of chaos theory. Better predictions of when a congestion will occur would allow measures to be taken to disperse it before it would have occurred. Combining chaos theory principles with a few other methods has led to a more accurate short-term prediction model, such as the [BML traffic model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BML_traffic_model).[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-148)

Chaos theory has been applied to environmental [water cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_cycle) data (also [hydrological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrological) data), such as rainfall and streamflow.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-149) These studies have yielded controversial results, because the methods for detecting a chaotic signature are often relatively subjective. Early studies tended to "succeed" in finding chaos, whereas subsequent studies and meta-analyses called those studies into question and provided explanations for why these datasets are not likely to have low-dimension chaotic dynamics.[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaos_theory#cite_note-150)

Lyapunov exponent was first published in 1897 in french, and later in 1907, is not mentioned by hadamard 1898 which derives a [hill differential equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_differential_equation) with negative curvature, and therefore a hyperbolic geometry in phase space, i.e. divergent orbits, but does not actually compute any exponent. In this PRL paper instead the lyapunov exponent is explicit.

- [High Anxieties — The Mathematics of Chaos](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5pKrKdNclYs) (2008) BBC documentary directed by [David Malone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Malone_(independent_filmmaker))
- [The chaos theory of evolution](https://www.newscientist.com/article/mg20827821.000-the-chaos-theory-of-evolution.html) – article published in Newscientist featuring similarities of evolution and non-linear systems including fractal nature of life and chaos.
- Jos Leys, [Étienne Ghys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Ghys) et Aurélien Alvarez, [*Chaos, A Mathematical Adventure*](https://www.chaos-math.org/en.html). Nine films about dynamical systems, the butterfly effect and chaos theory, intended for a wide audience.
- ["Chaos Theory"](https://www.bbc.co.uk/programmes/p00548f6), BBC Radio 4 discussion with Susan Greenfield, David Papineau & Neil Johnson (*In Our Time*, May 16, 2002)
- [Chaos: The Science of the Butterfly Effect](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fDek6cYijxI) ([Derek Muller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Muller) on YouTube, 2019)
